                                          Finite-Graph-Cover-Based
                                         Analysis of Factor Graphs in
                                            Classical and Quantum

                                        Information Processing Systems

                                                      HUANG, Yuwen


                                              A Thesis Submitted in Partial Fulfilment

                                                      Information Engineering




                                               The Chinese University of Hong Kong
                                                          September 2024
Abstract of thesis entitled: Finite-Graph-Cover-Based
Analysis of Factor Graphs in Classical and Quantum Information Pro-
cessing Systems
Submitted by HUANG, Yuwen
for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy
at The Chinese University of Hong Kong in September 2024



   Various intractable computational problems can be reduced to computing the
marginals and the partition function of a suitably defined multivariate function
represented by a factor graph. For computational problems relating to classical
statistical models, we consider standard factor graphs (S-FGs), i.e., factor graphs
with local functions taking on non-negative real values. For computational prob-
lems relating to quantum information processing systems, we consider so-called
double-edge factor graphs (DE-FGs), a class of factor graphs where local functions
take on complex values and satisfy some positive semi-definiteness constraints.
The sum-product algorithm (SPA) provides an efficient iterative method for ap-
proximating the marginals and the partition function induced by each of these
factor graphs.
   For an arbitrary S-FG, Yedidia et al. showed that fixed points of the SPA
on this S-FG correspond to stationary points of the Bethe free energy function.
Based on this, they defined the so-called Bethe approximation of the partition
function, or simply the Bethe partition function, in terms of the minimum of the
Bethe free energy function. Later, Vontobel provided a combinatorial character-
ization of the Bethe partition function in terms of the finite graph covers of the
S-FG. The proof of this characterization heavily relied on the method of types.
   In this thesis, we leverage finite graph covers to analyze the SPA and the Bethe
partition function for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. There are two main contributions
in this thesis.
   The first main contribution concerns a special class of S-FGs where the parti-
tion function of each S-FG equals the permanent of a nonnegative square matrix.

                                   i
The Bethe partition function for such an S-FG is called the Bethe permanent. A
combinatorial characterization of the Bethe permanent is given by the degree-M
Bethe permanent, which is defined based on the degree-M graph covers of the
underlying S-FG. In this thesis, we prove a degree-M-Bethe-permanent-based
lower bound on the permanent of a non-negative square matrix, resolving a con-
jecture proposed by Vontobel in [IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory, Mar. 2013]. We also
prove a degree-M-Bethe-permanent-based upper bound on the permanent of a
non-negative matrix. In the limit M → ∞, these lower and upper bounds yield
known Bethe-permanent-based lower and upper bounds on the permanent of a
non-negative square matrix. Similar results are obtained for another approxima-
tion of the permanent known as the (scaled) Sinkhorn permanent.
   The second main contribution is giving a combinatorial characterization of the
Bethe partition function for DE-FGs in terms of finite graph covers. In general,
approximating the partition function of a DE-FG is more challenging than for an
S-FG because the partition function of the DE-FG is a sum of complex values
and not just a sum of non-negative real values. Moreover, one cannot apply the
method of types for proving the combinatorial characterization as in the case of
S-FGs. We overcome this challenge by applying a suitable loop-calculus trans-
form (LCT). Originally, the LCT was introduced by Chertkov and Chernyak as
a special linear transform for S-FGs based on SPA fixed-point messages. Mori
further developed the LCT for S-FGs with non-binary alphabet, but his LCT was
limited to the case where SPA fixed-point messages consist of non-zero compo-
nents only. In contrast, the SPA on DE-FGs often involves messages containing
zeros. We introduce an alternative LCT that is applicable for both S-FGs and
DE-FGs and is without the limitation of non-zero components in SPA fixed-point
messages. Currently, we provide a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe
partition function in terms of finite graph covers for a class of DE-FGs satis-
fying an (easily checkable) condition. However, based on numerical results, we
suspect that this combinatorial characterization holds more broadly. We fur-
ther introduce the symmetric-subspace transform (SST), which reformulates the
previously mentioned combinatorial characterizations for S-FGs and DE-FGs in
terms of some integral. Both the LCT and the SST should be of interest beyond
proving the main results of this thesis.



                                            ii
摘要：

  各種棘手的計算問題可以歸結為計算由因子圖表示的適當定義的多變量函
數的邊際和配分函數。對於與經典統計模型相關的計算問題，我們考慮標準因
子圖（S-FG），即局部函數取非負實值的因子圖。對於與量子信息處理系統
相關的計算問題，我們考慮雙邊因子圖（DE-FG），這是一類局部函數取複數
值並滿足某些半正定約束的因子圖。乘積和算法（SPA）是一種高效的疊代方
法。它可以用來逼近由這些因子圖表示的邊際和配分函數。

  對於任意的S-FG，Yedidia等人顯示，SPA在此S-FG上的不動點對應於Bethe自
由能函數的駐點。基於此，他們定義了Bethe配分函數近似方法，由該近似方
法得到的函數我們簡稱為Bethe配分函數。Bethe配分函數是基於Bethe自由能
函數的最小值來定義的。基於S-FG的有限圖覆蓋，Vontobel提供了Bethe配分
函數的組合刻畫。這一刻畫的證明在很大程度上依賴於類型方法。

  在本論文中，我們利用有限圖覆蓋來分析S-FG和DE-FG的SPA和Bethe配分
函數。本論文有兩個主要貢獻。

  第一個主要貢獻涉及一類特殊的S-FG，其中每個S-FG的配分函數等於非
負方塊矩陣的積和式。這類S-FG的Bethe配分函數稱為Bethe積和式。Bethe積
和式的組合刻畫由度數為M的Bethe積和式給出。其中度數為M的Bethe積和式
是基於底層的S-FG的度數為M的圖覆蓋來定義的。在本論文中，我們證明了
一個基於度數為M的Bethe積和式的，對於非負方塊矩陣積和式的下界，解決
了Vontobel在[IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory, Mar. 2013]中提出的一個推測。我們
還證明了一個基於度數為M的Bethe積和式的，對於非負矩陣積和式的上界。
在M趨於無窮大的極限下，這些上下界會等於已知的，基於Bethe積和式的，
對於非負方塊矩陣積和式的上下界。類似的結果也適用於另一種對於積和式的
近似。這種近似被稱為（縮放的）Sinkhorn積和式。

  第二個主要貢獻是給出了基於DE-FG的有限圖覆蓋的，對於DE-FG的Bethe配
分函數的組合刻畫。一般來說，逼近DE-FG的配分函數比S-FG的配分函數更
具挑戰性，因為DE-FG的配分函數是複數的和，而不僅僅是非負實數的和。
此外，我們不能直接應用類型方法來證明該組合刻畫，這跟S-FG的情況不
一樣。我們通過對DE-FG應用適當的環計算變換（LCT）克服了這一困難。
最初，LCT由Chertkov和Chernyak引入，作為基於SPA不動點信息的應用在S-
FG上的特殊線性變換。Mori進一步發展了對於非二元字母表的S-FG的LCT，
但 他 提 出 的LCT僅 適 用 於SPA不 動 點 的 信 息 僅 包 含非 零 分量 的情 況。 相比
之 下 ，DE-FG上 的SPA通 常 涉 及 包 含 零 值 分 量的 信息 。基 于 此， 我們 拓展

                        iii
了Mori提出的LCT。我們引入了一種同時適用於S-FG和DE-FG的LCT，並且
我們提出的LCT也適用於SPA不動點信息中含有非零分量的情況。目前，我們
提供了對於一類滿足（易於檢查的）條件的DE-FG的Bethe配分函數的組合刻
畫。然而，基於數值仿真結果，我們推測這一組合刻畫對於廣泛的DE-FG都
是成立的。我們還進一步引入了對稱子空間變換（SST），這變換可以將前
述S-FG和DE-FG的組合刻畫重新表述為某些積分。除了用在證明本論文的主要
結果，LCT和SST應該會有更廣泛的應用。

possible without the invaluable support and guidance of numerous individuals. I
am deeply indebted to all those who have inspired, encouraged, and supported
me throughout my graduate studies.

Pascal O. Vontobel, for his unwavering guidance and support. His distinguished
contributions to information theory, especially his work on finite graph covers,
have not only inspired the research results presented in this thesis but have also
motivated me to pursue high-quality research throughout my graduate studies.
His dedication to my development as a scientist has been immeasurable. I am
particularly grateful for his insightful feedback and guidance on the finite-graph-
cover-based analysis presented in this paper. He has generously shared his exper-
tise, engaged in countless insightful discussions, and made great efforts to improve
my writing and presentation skills.

mation processing and theoretical computer science, have sparked new ideas and

Kashyap for sharing his unpublished notes, which have improved some of the
results presented in this thesis. The discussions with Yannan Wang and Haiwen

                           xiv
Chapter 1


Introduction

   Factor graphs are used for representing both classical statistical models [KFL01;
For01; Loe04] and quantum information processing systems [CV17; AA21]. For
classical statistical models, we consider standard factor graphs (S-FGs) [KFL01;
For01; Loe04], i.e., factor graphs with local functions taking on non-negative real
values. S-FGs have a wide range of applications across various fields, including
statistical mechanics (see, e.g., [MM09]), coding theory (see, e.g., [RU08]), and
communications (see, e.g., [Wym07]). For representing quantities of interests in
quantum information processing systems, we consider double-edges factor graphs
(DE-FGs) [CV17; Cao21], where local functions take complex values and have to
satisfy some positive semi-definiteness constraints. DE-FGs extend the concept
of S-FGs and are closely related to tensor networks formalism [CV09; Coe10;
RS19; AA21], which is a key research area in theoretical physics [Cow15].

   Many typical inference problems can be formulated as computing the marginals
and the partition function for a suitably defined multivariate function whose fac-
torization is depicted by an S-FG [MM09] or DE-FG [AA21]. Exact computation
of these quantities is, in general, challenging for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. In
order to efficiently approximate these quantities, a popular approach is to apply
the sum-product algorithm (SPA), also known as loopy belief propagation (LBP),
on S-FGs or DE-FGs. The SPA is a heuristic algorithm that has been success-
fully used for approximating the marginal functions and the partition functions
for various classes of S-FGs and DE-FGs.

   There are various characterizations of the pseudo-marginal functions obtained
via an SPA fixed point.     When an S-FG or DE-FG is cycle-free (i.e., tree-

                                        1
2                                                  CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION


structured), the pseudo-marginal functions evaluated at the SPA fixed point are
the exact marginal functions of the multivariate function represented by the S-
FG or DE-FG (see, e.g., [MM09]). For S-FGs or DE-FGs with cycles, the SPA
often provides surprisingly good approximations of the marginals and partition
functions. However, there exists instances of cyclic S-FGs and DE-FGs where the
SPA gives a poor approximations of these quantities [MWJ99; Hes03; WTSJ14].
    For S-FGs with cycles, Yedidia et al. in [YFW05] defined the Bethe free energy
function and also the Bethe approximation of the partition function, also known
as the Bethe partition function, in terms of the minimum of the Bethe free energy
function. The main idea of the Bethe free energy function dates back to the work
of Bethe in 1935 [Bet35]. Then Yedidia et al. [YFW05] provided a theoretical
justification of the SPA by showing that the fixed points of the SPA on an S-FG
correspond to the stationary points of the Bethe free energy function. Therefore,
the Bethe partition function can be computed with the help of the SPA.
    For S-FGs with cycles, Chertkov and Chernyak [CC06; CC07] introduced
a concept called loop calculus, which provides an expression that relates the
partition function to the Bethe partition function. This expression yields an
exact representation of the partition function as a finite sum of terms, with the
first term being the Bethe partition function and higher-order terms obtained
by adding so-called loop corrections. Mori in [Mor15b] extended the results by
Chertkov and Chernyak to S-FGs with non-binary alphabets, using concepts from
information geometry.
    Finite graph covers of an S-FG or DE-FG offer a different perspective to
understand the SPA on these graphs. A notable observation about finite graph
covers is that each cover looks locally the same as the base graph, i.e., the original
S-FG or DE-FG [KV03]. This implies that the SPA, a locally operating algorithm,
“cannot distinguish” whether it is running on the base graph, or implicitly on
any of the finite graph covers.
    Finite graph covers serves as a useful theoretical tool for analyzing the SPA
and the Bethe partition functions for S-FGs. Specifically:

    • In order to understand the performance of the SPA for decoding low-density
      parity-check (LDPC) codes, Koetter et al. [KV03; KLVW07] studied finite
      graph covers of S-FGs representing LDPC codes. Ruozzi et al. [RTT09]
                                                                                 3

     utilized finite graph covers to characterize the behavior of the max-product
     algorithm for Gaussian graphical models. (For Gaussian graphical models,
     the max-product algorithm is essentially equivalent to the SPA.)

   • Vontobel [Von13a] provided a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe
     partition function in terms of finite graph covers of an S-FG, as illustrated
     in Fig. 1.1. In Fig. 1.1, N denotes an arbitrary S-FG, Z(N) is the partition
     function, ZB (N) is the Bethe partition function, and ZB,M (N), the degree-
     M Bethe partition function, is defined based on the degree-M covers of N.
     Vontobel [Von13a] showed that

                                 ZB,M (N) = Z(N),      M = 1,
                         lim sup ZB,M (N) = ZB (N).
                          M →∞

     Similar characterizations were discussed in [AAL+17; ALP+22].

   • Based on Vontobel’s findings [Von13a], Ruozzi et al. in [Ruo12] and Csikvári
     et al. in [CRS22] proved that the partition functions are lower bounded by
     the Bethe partition functions for certain classes of S-FGs, particularly re-
     solving a conjecture by Sudderth et al. [EMA07] regarding log-supermodular
     graphical models.

   In this thesis, we also leverage finite graph covers to analyze the SPA and the
relationship between the Bethe partition functions and the partition functions for
both S-FGs and DE-FGs. Our investigation yields two main contributions:

  1. Degree-M-Bethe-permanent-based bounds for the permanent
     of a non-negative square matrix

     Vontobel in [Von13b] introduced a class of S-FGs such that the partition
     function of each S-FG is equal to the permanent of a non-negative square
4                                                  CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION


      matrix. The combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition func-
      tion for such an S-FG is given by the degree-M Bethe permanent, which
      is defined based on the degree-M covers of the S-FG. In this thesis, we
      present degree-M-Bethe-permanent-based bounds on the permanent of a
      non-negative square matrix, resolving a conjecture proposed by Vontobel
      in [Von13b, Conjecture 51] Detailed discussion can be found in Section 1.1.

    2. Combinatorial characterization of Bethe partition function
      for DE-FGs

      For an arbitrary DE-FG, a natural question is whether there is a combina-
      torial characterization of its Bethe partition function in terms of its finite
      graph covers. In this thesis, we prove this characterization for DE-FGs that
      satisfy an easily checkable condition. However, based on the obtained nu-
      merical results, we suspect that this characterization holds more broadly.
      The detailed discussion is in Section 1.2.


1.1     Degree-M Bethe and Sinkhorn Permanent Based Bounds
        on the Permanent of a Non-negative square Matrix

    This section establishes finite-graph-cover-based bounds for the permanent of
a nonnegative square matrix. In particular, Sections 1.1.1 and 1.1.2 introduce
the permanent of a non-negative square matrix and the Bethe permanent, re-
spectively, with the latter being an S-FG-based approximation of the matrix per-
manent. Section 1.1.3 presents the degree-M Bethe permanent, which is defined
based on degree-M covers of the underlying S-FG. Section 1.1.4 then establishes
degree-M-Bethe-permanent-based bounds on the matrix permanent. Moreover,
Sections 1.1.2–1.1.4 show that similar results holds for the (scaled) Sinkhorn per-
manent [ACSS21], which is another S-FG-based approximation of the matrix
permanent.


1.1.1    The Permanent of a Square Matrix

    For any n ∈ Z≥1 , define [n] , {1, 2, . . . , n} and let S[n] be the set of all n!
bijections from [n] to [n]. Recall the definition of the permanent of a real square
matrix (see, e.g., [MM84]).
1.1. FINITE-GRAPH-COVER-BASED BOUNDS ON THE PERMANENT                            5

                                             
Definition 1. Let n ∈ Z≥1 and let θ , θ(i, j) i,j∈[n] be a real matrix of size
n × n. The permanent of θ is defined to be
                                        X Y                    
                           perm(θ) ,                  θ i, σ(i) .             (1.1)
                                       σ∈S[n] i∈[n]


                                                                                △

   In this thesis, we consider only the matrices θ such that there is at least one
                    Q           
σ ∈ S[n] such that     θ i, σ(i) 6= 0.
                   i∈[n]
   Computing the exact permanent is in the complexity class #P, where #P is
the set of the counting problems associated with the decision problems in the
class NP. Notably, already the computation of the permanent of matrices that
contain only zeros and ones is #P-complete [Val79].


1.1.2   The Bethe Permanent and the (Scaled) Sinkhorn Permanent of
        a Non-negative Square Matrix

   While being able to compute the exact permanent of a matrix might be desir-
able in some circumstances, many applications require only a good approximation
of the permanent. In this thesis, we focus on the important special case of ap-
proximating the permanent of a non-negative real matrix, i.e., a matrix where
all entries are non-negative real numbers. In particular, we study two graphical-
model-based methods for approximating the permanent of a non-negative square
matrix. The first method is motivated by Bethe-approximation / SPA based
methods proposed in [CKV08; HJ09] and studied in more detail in [Von13b]. The
second method is motivated by Sinkhorn’s matrix scaling algorithm [LSW00].
   The main idea of these methods is to construct an S-FG [For01; Loe04] whose
partition function equals the permanent. Consequently, the permanent can be
obtained by finding the minimum of the Gibbs free energy function associated
with the factor graph. However, because finding the minimum of the Gibbs
free energy function is in general intractable unless n is small, one considers the
minimization of some functions that suitably approximate the Gibbs free energy
function.
   A first approximation of the Gibbs free energy function is given by the Bethe
free energy function [YFW05]; the resulting approximation of perm(θ) is called
the Bethe permanent and denoted by permB (θ). Vontobel [Von13b] showed that
6                                                  CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION


for the relevant factor graph, the Bethe free energy function is convex and that
the SPA converges to its minimum. (This is in contrast to the situation for factor
graphs in general, where the Bethe free energy function associated with a factor
graph is not convex and the SPA is not guaranteed to converge to its minimum.)
The Bethe permanent permB (θ) satisfies


                                    perm(θ)
                              1≤              ≤ 2n/2 .                         (1.2)
                                    permB (θ)


The first inequality in (1.2) was proven by Gurvits [Gur11] with the help of an
inequality of Schrijver [Sch98]. Later on, an alternative proof based on results
from real stable polynomials was given by Straszak and Vishnoi in [SV19] and
Anari and Gharan in [AG21]. The second inequality in (1.2) was conjectured
by Gurvits [Gur11] and proven by Anari and Rezaei [AR19]. Both inequalities
in (1.2) are the best possible given only the knowledge of the size of the matrix θ.

    A second approximation of the Gibbs free energy function is given by the
Sinkhorn free energy function; the resulting approximation of perm(θ) is called
the Sinkhorn permanent and denoted by permS (θ). (The Sinkhorn free energy
function was formally defined in [Von14] based on some considerations in [HJ09].
Note that permS (θ) appears already in [LSW00] as an approximation of perm(θ)
without being called the Sinkhorn permanent.) The Sinkhorn free energy func-
tion is easily seen to be convex and it can be efficiently minimized with the
help of Sinkhorn’s matrix scaling algorithm [LSW00], thereby motivating its
name. In this thesis, we will actually work with the scaled Sinkhorn permanent
permscS (θ) , e−n · permS (θ), a variant of the Sinkhorn permanent permS (θ) that
was introduced in [AG21]. The scaled Sinkhorn permanent permscS (θ) satisfies


                                  n!    perm(θ)
                           en ·    n
                                     ≤             ≤ en .                      (1.3)
                                  n    permscS (θ)


The first inequality in (1.3) follows from van der Waerden’s inequality proven
by Egorychev [Ego81] and Falikman [Fal81], whereas the second equality follows
from a relatively simple to prove inequality. Both inequalities in (1.3) are the
best possible given only the knowledge of the size of the matrix θ. Note that the
                                            √
value on the LHS of (1.3) is approximately 2πn.

1.1.3      A Combinatorial Characterization of the Bethe Permanent and
           the (Scaled) Sinkhorn Permanent

       While the definition of perm(θ) in (1.1) clearly has a combinatorial flavor
(e.g., counting weighted perfect matchings in a complete bipartite graph with
two times n vertices), it is, a priori, not clear if a combinatorial characterization
can be given for permB (θ) and permscS (θ). However, this is indeed the case.
       Namely, using the results in [Von13a], a finite-graph-cover-based combinatorial
characterization was given for permB (θ) in [Von13b]. Namely,

                               permB (θ) = lim sup permB,M (θ),

    These combinatorial characterizations of the Bethe and scaled Sinkhorn per-

    Besides the quantities permB,M (θ) and permscS,M (θ) being of inherent interest,
they are particularly interesting toward understanding the ratios

                               perm(θ)            perm(θ)
                                         ,
                               permB (θ)         permscS (θ)

for general non-negative square matrices or for special classes of non-negative
square matrices.
    For example, as discussed in [Von13a, Sec. VII], one can consider the equation

              perm(θ)      perm(θ) permB,2 (θ) permB,3 (θ)
                        =            ·           ·            ··· .
              permB (θ)   permB,2 (θ) permB,3 (θ) permB,4 (θ)

If one can give bounds on the ratios appearing on the RHS of this equation, then
one obtains bounds on the ratio on the LHS of this equation.
    Alternatively, one can consider the equation

                        perm(θ)     perm(θ) permB,2 (θ)
                                 =            ·           .                         (1.4)
                       permB (θ)   permB,2 (θ) permB (θ)
                       | {z } |        {z    } |  {z    }
                           ➀                 ➁            ➂


Based on numerical and analytical results, Ng and Vontobel [NV22] concluded
that it appears that for many classes of matrices of interest, the ratio ➁ behaves
similarly to the ratio ➂. Therefore, understanding the ratio ➁ goes a long way
toward understanding the ratio ➀. (Note that the ratio ➁ is easier to analyze
than the ratio ➀ for some classes of matrices of interest.)
    Results similar in spirit to the above results were given for the Bethe approx-
imation of the partition function of other graphical models (see, e.g., [Ruo12;
Von16; CRS22]).

         Let us put the above results into some context:

     • The first inequality in (1.8) proves the first (weaker) conjecture in [Von13b,
           Conjecture 52].

     • Smarandache and Haenggi [SH15] claimed a proof of the second (stronger)
           conjecture in [Von13b, Conjecture 52],3 which would imply the first (weaker)
           conjecture in [Von13b, Conjecture 52]. However, the proof in [SH15] is
           incomplete.

     • The first inequality in (1.10) proves the first (weaker) conjecture in [Von13b,
           Conjecture 51].

     • The proof of the first inequality in (1.8), and with that the proof of the
           first inequality in (1.10), uses an inequality of Schrijver [Sch98]. By now,
           there are various proofs of Schrijver’s inequality and also various proofs of
           the first inequality in (1.2) available in the literature, however, the ones
           that we are aware of are either quite involved (like Schrijver’s original proof
           of his inequality) or use reasonably sophisticated machinery like real stable
           polynomials. Given the established veracity of the first inequality in (1.10),

      the other inequalities in (1.10) and (1.11).

      In fact, for M = 2, a basic proof of the inequalities in (1.10) is available: it
      is based on [NV22, Proposition 2] (which appears in this thesis as Propo-
      sition 70), along with the observation that the quantity c(σ1 , σ2 ) appearing
      therein is bounded as 0 ≤ c(σ1 , σ2 ) ≤ n/2. Another basic proof of the first
      inequality in (1.10) is based on a straightforward generalization of the result
      in [Csi17, Lemma 4.2] from {0, 1}-valued matrices to arbitrary non-negative
      square matrices and then averaging over 2-covers.

      where M ∈ Z≥1 is arbitrary, a basic proof of the first inequality in (1.10)

      to generalize this proof to other matrices θ.

      toward a better understanding of the Bethe approximation of the partition
      function of other factor graphs. (For more on this topic, see the discussion
      in Chapter 7.)



1.2    Characterizing the Bethe Partition Function of
       Double-Edges Factor Graphs via Graph Covers

   The results in Section 1.1 are for factor graphs called S-FGs, i.e., for fac-
tor graphs with local functions taking on non-negative real values. Recently,
several papers [LV12; LV17; LV20; Mor15a] have explored more general factor
graphs, in particular those where the local functions take on complex values.
This exploration is particularly relevant for representing quantities of interest
in quantum information processing. (For connections of these factor graphs to
12                                                                  CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION


other graphical notations in physics, see [LV17, Appendix A].) The structure of
these factor graphs is not completely arbitrary. In order to formalize such fac-
tor graphs, Cao and Vontobel [CV17] introduced DE-FGs, where local functions
take on complex values and have to satisfy certain positive semi-definiteness con-
straints. They [CV17] further showed that the partition function of a DE-FG is
a non-negative real number. However, because the partition function is the sum
of complex numbers (notably, the real and the imaginary parts of these com-
plex numbers can have a positive or negative sign), approximating the partition
function is, in general, much more challenging for DE-FGs than for S-FGs. This
problem is known as the numerical sign problem in applied mathematics and
theoretical physics (see, e.g., [LGS+90]).
         Based on the positive semi-definiteness constraints posed for DE-FGs, Cao
and Vontobel [Cao21] developed the SPA for DE-FGs, where the SPA messages
also satisfy these constraints, and further defined the Bethe partition function
                                                             4
based on the SPA fixed-point messages.                           Then they showed that the Bethe
partition function is a non-negative real number.
         DE-FGs have a strong connection to tensor networks formulism [CV09; Coe10;
RS19; AA21], which has become a central topic in theoretical physics [Cow15].
In [AA21], Alkabetz and Arad showed how to map a projected-entangled-pair-
state (PEPS) tensor network to a DE-FG. Alkabetz and Arad in [AA21] connected
the SPA for DE-FGs to tensor network contractions. Motivated by this connec-
tion, Tindall and Fishman [TF23] related the SPA for DE-FGs to known tensor
network gauging methods. Moreover, Tindall et al. [TFSS24] showed that for the
kicked Ising quantum system studied in [KEA+23], the SPA-based approxima-
tion results are more accurate and precise than those obtained from the quantum
processor and many other classical methods. These promising results motivate
us to study the SPA for DE-FGs.
         Given that both the partition function and the Bethe partition function are
non-negative real numbers for an arbitrary DE-FG [CV17], the existence of a

     4
         Similar to the definition of the Bethe partition function in [CV17, Def. 8], in this thesis, the Bethe
partition function is formulated based on the so-called pseudo-dual Bethe free energy function for S-
FGs. Although generalizing the primal Bethe free energy function from S-FGs to DE-FGs is formally
straightforward, it poses challenges because of the multi-valuedness of the complex logarithm and is
left for future research.
1.2. THE GRAPH COVERS FOR DE-FGS                                                          13


combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite
graph covers for DE-FGs remains an open question. Numerical results on small
DE-FGs suggested that such a characterization might exist. However, because
in general, the partition function of a DE-FG is a sum of complex numbers,
proving the combinatorial characterization for DE-FGs turns out to be much more
challenging than for S-FGs. The method of types, which was the main ingredient
in the proof of the combinatorial characterization for S-FGs [Von13a], cannot be
directly adapted to the proof for DE-FGs. The challenges of approximating the
sum of complex numbers are illustrated by the following example.

Example 6. For any positive-valued integer M and any complex number α, we

We can see that for the case 0 ≤ α ≤ 1, all the terms in ZM are non-negative and
are added up constructively, allowing us to approximate ZM by the term with the
largest magnitude. Following a similar idea, Vontobel [Von13a] used the method
of types to characterize the valid configurations in finite graph covers of an S-
FG, and he further gave a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition
function of the S-FG.
         However, if α is a complex-valued number with non-zero imaginary part, the
terms in the expression of ZM in (1.15), might take values in the field of complex
numbers, and these terms are added up constructively and destructively. In this
case, we cannot approximate lim M −1 · log(ZM ) using the same idea as in the
                                          M →∞
case 0 ≤ α ≤ 1, as the term with the largest magnitude gives a poor estimate of
ZM .


1.2.1          Main Contributions

         We develop a novel approach to give a combinatorial characterization of the
Bethe partition functions for a certain class of DE-FGs. This novel proof approach
is one of the main results in this thesis, besides the statement itself.
         Our proof approach consists of three main ingredients:

     1. At the first step, we apply the loop-calculus transform (LCT)5 to a DE-FG
           based on a fixed point of the SPA. (If the SPA has multiple fixed points, we
           assume that it is the fixed point with the largest Bethe partition function
           value.) The benefit of the LCT is that the transformed SPA fixed-point
           messages have an elementary form, which is independent of the edge and
           the direction of a message. Note that the LCT leaves the partition function
           of the DE-FG unchanged.

     2. At the second step, for any positive-valued integer M, we construct the
           average degree-M cover for the transformed DE-FG. Then we express the
     5
         The loop-calculus transform can be seen as a particular instance of a holographic transform [AM11].
1.3. STRUCTURE OF THIS THESIS                                                   15


      average of the partition functions of the degree-M covers in terms of the
      partition function of this average degree-M cover.

  3. At the third step, we evaluate the partition function of this average degree-
      M cover in the limit M → ∞. So far, we can relate the limit of this partition
      function to the Bethe partition function when some (easily checkable) con-
      ditions are satisfied. However, based on the obtained numerical results, we
      suspect that this combinatorial characterization holds more generally. We
      leave it as an open problem to broadly characterize when the DE-FGs do
      not satisfy the (easily checkable) conditions.

   Based on finite graph covers of a DE-FG, we further develop the symmetric-
subspace transform (SST), which allows one to express the partition function of
the average degree-M cover of the DE-FG in terms of some integral. Both the
LCT and the SST should be of interest beyond proving the main results in this
thesis. The reasons are listed as follows.

  1. The LCT introduced in this thesis is based on SPA fixed-point messages
      consisting of complex-valued components, which is applicable for both S-
      FGs and DE-FGs. This contrasts with the LCT proposed in [Mor15b],
      which requires SPA fixed-point messages to be non-zero valued. Therefore,
      our proposed transform generalizes the one presented in [Mor15b].

  2. The SST defined in this thesis is applicable for both S-FGs and DE-FGs.
      To the best of our knowledge, it is the first time that this transform is
      introduced in factor-graph literature, although it has wide applications in
      quantum physics [WBC15; Har13].


1.3    Structure of this Thesis

   This thesis is structured as follows. Chapter 2 reviews the basic definitions
and notations that are frequently used in this thesis. In particular, Chapter 2
introduces S-FGs, a special class of S-FGs such that the partition function of
each S-FG is equal to the permanent of a non-negative square matrix, DE-FGs,
and finite graph covers of these factor graphs. Chapter 3 proves degree-M-Bethe-
permanent-based bounds on the permanent of a non-negative square matrix and
16                                               CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION


also similar results for another approximation of the permanent known as the
(scaled) Sinkhorn permanent. Chapters 4 and 5 introduce the LCT and the SST,
respectively, for both S-FGs and DE-FGs. Chapter 6 proves the graph-cover
theorem, i.e., a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in
terms of finite graph covers, for a class of DE-FGs satisfying an easily checkable
condition. Various (longer) proofs have been collected in the appendices.
Chapter 2


Preliminaries

   In this chapter, we introduce the preliminaries of this thesis. We begin by
introducing the basic definitions and notations used throughout this thesis in
Section 2.1. Next, we review the fundamental concepts of standard normal factor
graphs (S-NFGs) in Section 2.2. In Section 2.3, we study a class of S-NFGs such
that the partition function of each of these S-NFGs equals the permanent of a
non-negative square matrix. Moving to the quantum case, we introduce double-
edge normal factor graphs (DE-NFGs) in Section 2.5. Finally, in Section 2.6, we
present the finite graph covers of the factor graphs introduced in this chapter.



2.1    Basic Definition and Notations

   If not mentioned otherwise, all variable alphabets are assumed to be finite.
We will use, without essential loss of generality, normal factor graphs (NFGs),
i.e., factor graphs where variables are associated with edges [For01; Loe04].

2.2         Standard Normal Factor Graphs (S-NFGs)

       In this section, we review some basic concepts and properties of an S-NFG.
We use an example to introduce the key concepts of an S-NFG.
2.2. STANDARD NORMAL FACTOR GRAPHS (S-NFGS)                                                                19


                                                f1     x1       f2


                                              x2     x3           x4


                                                       x5
                                                f3              f4

                                         Figure 2.1: NFG N in Example 7.



Example 7. Consider the multivariate function

          g(x1 , . . . , x5 ) , f1 (x1 , x2 , x3 ) · f2 (x1 , x4 ) · f3 (x2 , x5 ) · f4 (x3 , x4 , x5 ).

where g, the so-called global function, is defined to be the product of the so-called
local functions f1 , f2 , f3 and f4 . We can visualize the factorization of g with
the help of the S-NFG N in Fig. 2.1. Note that the S-NFG N shown in Fig. 2.1
consists of four function nodes f1 , . . . , f4 and five (full) edges with associated
variables x1 , . . . , x5 .                                                                                

    An edge that connects to two function nodes is called a full edge, whereas an
edge that is connected to only one function node is called a half edge. For the
purposes of this thesis, it is sufficient to consider only S-NFGs with full edges,
because S-NFGs with half edges can be turned into S-NFGs with only full edges
by attaching a dummy 1-valued function node to the other end of every half edge
without changing any marginals or the partition function.

Definition 8. An S-NFG N(F , E, X ) consists of the following objects:

   1. The graph (F , E) with vertex set F and edge set E, where F is also known
       as the set of function nodes. (As mentioned above, every edge e ∈ E will be
       assumed to be a full edge connecting two function nodes.)
                                   Q
   2. The alphabet X ,                   Xe , where Xe is the alphabet of the variable xe as-
                                   e∈E
       sociated with the edge e ∈ E. In this thesis, without loss of generality, we
       suppose that 0 ∈ Xe for all e ∈ E.

For a given S-NFG N(F , E, X ), we fix the order of the function nodes by F =
{f1 , . . . , f|F | }. Then we make the following definitions.

   3. For every function node f ∈ F , the set ∂f is defined to be the set of edges
       incident on f .
20                                                                CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARIES


     4. For every edge e = (fi , fj ) ∈ E, the pair (fi , fj ) is defined to be the pair
           of function nodes that are connected to edge e such that i < j. The set ∂e
           is defined to be the set of function nodes incident on e, i.e., ∂e , {fi , fj }.
           Note that for notational convenience, here we impose a direction on every
           edge (fi , fj ), i.e., the inequality i < j, that is irrelevant for our results.

     5. For any finite set I ⊆ E, we define

                                                  

Proof. See, e.g., [Von13a, Lemma 8].                                                  

      In general, neither the direct evaluation of the partition function nor solving
the convex optimization problem in Proposition 10 leads to a tractable compu-
tational problem, except for small-scaled S-NFG. This limitation motivates the
study of alternative approaches, specifically approximations of the partition func-
tion and the Gibbs free energy function.
      In the following, we introduce the SPA, whose fixed points often provide decent
approximation to the partition function. Note that we only give the technical
details of the SPA on an S-NFG; for a general discussion with respect to (w.r.t.)
the motivations behind the SPA, see, e.g., [KFL01; Loe04].

Definition 11. (The SPA on S-NFG) Consider some S-NFG N. The SPA is an
iterative algorithm that sends messages along edges, where messages are functions

     Now we introduce the Bethe free energy function, which gives an S-FG-based
approximation to the Gibbs free energy function. Proposition 10 states that the
value of the partition function can be evaluated by the minimum of the Gibbs
free energy function. Therefore, the minimum of the Bethe free energy function
yields an approximation to the partition function. The following definitions are
adapted from [YFW05, Section V] to S-NFGs.
2.2. STANDARD NORMAL FACTOR GRAPHS (S-NFGS)                                             25

lection of the beliefs β is defined in (2.7). The local marginal polytope B(N)
associated with N is defined to be the following set:

Definition 16. [YFW05, Section V] Let N be some S-NFG. The Bethe free energy
function is defined to be the following mapping:

                     FB (β) : B(N) → R,       β 7→ UB (β) − HB (β),

where
                                              X
              UB (β) : B(N) → R,       β 7→       UB,f (βf ),
                                              f
                                              X                   X
             HB (β) : B(N) → R,        β 7→       HB,f (βf ) −          HB,e (βe ),
                                              f                     e

with
                                                  X                             
            UB,f (βf ) : ΠX∂f → R,     βf 7→ −          βf (x∂f ) · log f (x∂f ) ,
                                                  x∂f
                                                  X                              
            HB,f (βf ) : ΠX∂f → R,     βf 7→ −          βf (x∂f ) · log βf (x∂f ) ,
                                                  x∂f
                                                  X                            
             HB,e (βe ) : ΠXe → R,     βe 7→ −          βe (xe ) · log βe (xe ) .
                                                  xe

Here, UB is the Bethe average energy function and HB is the Bethe entropy func-
                                                               P
tion. (Note that for NFGs with full and half edges, the sum in   HB,e (βe ) is only
                                                                            e
over all full edges.) The Bethe approximation of the partition function, henceforth
called the Bethe partition function, is defined to be
                                                     
                         ZB (N) , exp − min FB (β) .                                  (2.9)
                                               β∈B(N)

This definition is often viewed as the primal formulation of the Bethe partition
function.                                                                               △

Proposition 17. Consider N to be cycle-free, i.e., tree-structured.
26                                                    CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARIES


     1. [YFW05, Proposition 3] It holds that ZB (N) = Z(N).

     2. (See, e.g., [JI08, Section 2.5].) The SPA on N converges to a unique fixed
        point and the beliefs evaluated by the SPA fixed-point messages, as defined
        in Definition 12, is at the location of the minimum of the Bethe free energy
        function, i.e., the value of the Bethe partition function can be evaluated by
        the fixed-point messages.

By these two properties, we know that for a cycle-free S-NFG N, the value of the
partition function can be evaluated by the SPA fixed-point messages.               

Remark 18. Consider some S-NFG N with cycles. Suppose that we get an SPA
fixed-point message vector µ and obtain a collection of the beliefs β based on µ,
following Definition 12. One can verify that

                                        β ∈ B(N).
                                                
Therefore, the value of the function exp −FB (β) , which is obtained by µ, can
be viewed as an approximation of the value of the Bethe partition function.

     For an S-NFG with cycles, the behavior of the SPA varies and the Bethe par-
tition function provides an approximation of the partition function. One of the
main results in the paper by Yedidia et al. [YFW05, Section V] is a characteri-
zation of the stationary points of the Bethe free energy function in terms of the
SPA fixed-point messages.

Theorem 19. Consider some S-NFG N.

     1. [YFW05, Theorem 2] If the stationary point of the Bethe free energy function
        is in the interior of the LMP, i.e.,

                                                 |X   |
                            β ∈ B(N),     βf ∈ R>0∂f ,    f ∈ F,

        then this stationary point corresponds to an SPA fixed point of N.

     2. [YFW05, Theorem 3] If all the local functions in N are positive-valued, i.e.,

                           f (x∂f ) ∈ R>0 ,    x∂f ∈ X∂f , f ∈ F ,

        then each of the local minima of the Bethe free energy function corresponds
        to an SPA fixed point of N.
2.2. STANDARD NORMAL FACTOR GRAPHS (S-NFGS)                                     27


                                                                                 

   For S-NFG N with cycles, the SPA on N might converge to a fixed point, or
it might fail to converge. The Bethe partition function ZB (N) can be smaller,
equal, or larger than the partition function ZB (N).

  1. In [Ruo12, Corollary 4.2], Ruozzi proved that the Bethe partition function
     lower bounds the partition function for S-NFGs whose global functions are a
     binary and log-supermodular function. In subsequent work [Ruo13], Ruozzi
     extended this lower bound to certain classes of S-NFGs whose global func-
     tions are not necessarily binary or log-supermodular, including S-NFGs for
     ferromagnetic Potts models with a uniform external field [Ruo13, Theorems
     5.2 and 5.4] and its generalizations [Ruo13, Theorem 5.6], as well as a class
     of S-NFGs related to the problem of counting weighted graph homomor-
     phisms. [Ruo13, Theorem 6.2].

  2. In [Von13b], Vontobel investigated the S-NFG whose partition function
     equals the permanent of a non-negative square matrix. For such S-NFG,
     he showed that the Bethe free energy function is a convex function and the
     SPA finds the minimum of the Bethe free energy function exponentially fast.
     Huang and Vontobel [HV24] extended Vontobel’s results to a broader class
     of bipartite S-FGs where each local function is defined based on a (possi-
     bly different) multi-affine homogeneous real stable (MAHRS) polynomial.
     They further showed that a certain projection of the local marginal poly-
     tope equals the convex hull of the set of valid configurations, the Bethe free
     energy function possesses some convexity properties, and, for the typical
     case where the S-FG has an SPA fixed point consisting of positive-valued
     messages only, the SPA finds the value of the Bethe partition function ex-
     ponentially fast.

  3. Gurvits in [Gur11, Theorem 2.2] proved that for the S-NFG studied in [Von13b],
     the Bethe partition function lower bounds the partition function, i.e., he
     proved the lower bound in (1.2). Subsequent work by Straszak and Vish-
     noi [SV19, Theorem 3.2] and Anari and Gharan [AG21, Theorem 1.1] ex-
     tended this lower bound to the S-NFGs where each local function corre-
     sponds to a (possibly different) multi-affine real stable polynomial. (Note
28                                                                      CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARIES


           that they did not assume homogenity.) The class of the S-NFGs consid-
           ered in [SV19; AG21] is a generalization of the class of the S-NFGs studied
           in [Von13b; HV24].

         Besides bounding the partition function using the Bethe partition function, we
can also connect these two functions via finite graph covers. Unlike the analytical
definition of the Bethe partition function in (2.9), Vontobel in [Von13a] provided
a combinatorial characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite
graph covers. A key step in achieving this characterization was to use the method
of types to characterize valid configurations in M-covers. This characterization is
illustrated in Fig. 1.1. For M being one, the degree-M Bethe partition function
is equal to the partition function. As M grows to infinity, the limit superior of
the degree-M Bethe partition function is equal to the Bethe partition function.
Section 2.6 provides technical details about M-covers and the degree-M Bethe
partition function.
         Given that the Bethe partition function is closely related to the SPA, in the
following, we give a pseudo-dual formulation of the Bethe partition function based
on the SPA fixed-point message vectors.

Remark 21. Let us make some remarks on the pseudo-dual formulation of the
Bethe partition function in Definition 20.

  1. Consider an arbitrary cycle-free S-NFG N. Proposition 17 states that the
     SPA on N converges to a unique fixed point and that ZB (N) = Z(N). Denote
     the associated SPA fixed-point message vector by µ. One can verify that
     ZB,SPA (N, µ) = Z(N).

                                                                          ∗
  2. By Assumption 13, in this thesis, we only consider the S-NFGs where ZB,SPA (N),
     as defined in Definition 20, is well-defined, i.e., the SPA fixed-point mes-
     sages µ such that

                                  ∗
                 ZB,SPA (N, µ) = ZB,SPA (N),            Ze (µ) > 0,        e ∈ E.

     See Item 3 for a non-trivial S-NFG where this condition is not satisfied.

  3. Let us consider the S-NFG in Fig. 2.2, where the dots are used for denoting
     the row index of the matrices f1 and f2 w.r.t. the function nodes f1 and f2 ,
     respectively. In particular, these matrices are given by
                                                                       
             f1 , f1 (x1 , x2 ) x1 ∈X1 ,x2 ∈X2 ,      f2 , f2 (x1 , x2 ) x1 ∈X1 ,x2 ∈X2

     with row indexed by x1 and column indexed by x2 . Running the SPA on this
     S-NFG is equivalent to applying the power method to the matrix f1 · f2T . If
30                                                CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARIES


       we set

       We cannot evaluate ZB,SPA (N, µ) for this S-NFG N.

                                                          ∗
     4. [YFW05, Section VI-C] A sufficient condition for ZB,SPA (N) being well-
       defined for the S-NFG N is that the local functions in N satisfy

                           f (x∂f ) > 0,    x∂f ∈ X∂f , f ∈ F .

       For such S-NFG N, both the SPA fixed-point messages and the beliefs at the
       locations of the local minima of the Bethe free energy function are positive-
                                  ∗
       valued. Thus the function ZB,SPA (N) is well-defined. The statement proven
       by Yedidia et al. in [YFW05, Theorem 3] further implied that
                                             ∗

     5. Consider running the SPA defined in Definition 11 on some S-NFG N. Sup-
       pose that we obtain an SPA fixed-point message vector µ. Following Defi-
       nition 12, we obtain the collection of the beliefs β ∈ B(N), which is induced
       by µ. Then we relate the value of the Bethe free energy function evaluated
       at β to the value of the µ-based Bethe partition function by the following
       equation:
                                         

2.3      The S-NFG N(θ) for the Permanent of a Non-negative
         Square Matrix θ

     The main purpose of this section is to present an S-NFG whose partition
function equals the permanent of a non-negative matrix θ of size n × n. There
2.3. THE S-NFG FOR THE MATRIX PERMANENT                                           31


are different ways to develop such S-NFGs [GJR10; Von13b]. (For a comparison
between the ways in [GJR10; Von13b], see[Von13b, Section VII-E].) In this thesis,
we consider the S-NFG N(θ) defined in [Von13b]. Throughout this section, let θ
be a fixed non-negative matrix of size n × n.

Definition 22. We define the S-NFG N(θ) , N(F , E, X ) as follows (see also

2.4         Free Energy Functions Associated with N(θ)

       We introduce various free energy functions associated with N(θ). First we
introduce the Gibbs free energy function, whose minimum yields perm(θ). After-
wards, we introduce the Bethe free energy function and the scaled Sinkhorn free
energy function, which are approximations of the Gibbs free energy functions.
Their minima yield the Bethe permanent permB (θ) and the scaled Sinkhorn
permanent permscS (θ), respectively, which are approximations of perm(θ). Fi-
nally, we introduce the degree-M Bethe permanent permB,M (θ) and the degree-M
scaled Sinkhorn permanent permscS,M (θ).

Definition 25. Consider the S-NFG N(θ). The Gibbs free energy function asso-
ciated with N(θ) (see [YFW05, Section III]) is defined to be

                                 FG,θ : ΠA(θ) → R
                                               p 7→ UG,θ (p) − HG (p)

with
                                                X                           

                                                X                           
                               HG (p) , −                p(Pσ ) · log p(Pσ ) ,
                                              Pσ ∈P[n]


where UG,θ and HG are called the Gibbs average energy function and the Gibbs
entropy function, respectively.6                                                                       △

       One can verify that
                                                         
                                         

                                                         p∈ΠA(θ)


       Recall the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem (see, e.g., [HJ12, Theorem 4.3.49]),
which states that the convex hull of all permutation matrices of size n × n equals
the set of all doubly stochastic matrices of size n × n. Because p ∈ ΠA(θ) is a
probability mass function over the set of all permutation matrices of size n × n
with support contained in the support of θ, the Gibbs free energy function can
be reformulated as follows as a function over Γn (θ) instead of over ΠA(θ) .
   6
       Note that P[n] ⊆ X , where the configuration set X of N(θ) was defined in Definition 22. With this,
Pσ ∈ P[n] is a configuration of N(θ) and g(Pσ ) is well defined.

         However, neither formulation of the Gibbs free energy function leads to a
tractable optimization problem in general unless n is small. This motivates the
study of approximations of the permanent based on approximating the Gibbs
free energy function, especially based on approximating the modified Gibbs free
energy function in the reformulation presented in Definition 26. In the following,
we will first discuss an approximation known as the Bethe free energy function
and then an approximation known as the scaled Sinkhorn free energy function.
         In general, the Bethe free energy function associated with an S-NFG is a
function over the so-called local marginal polytope associated with the S-NFG.
(For a general discussion of the local marginal polytope associated with an S-
NFG, see, e.g., [JI08, Section 4].) The thesis [Von13b, Section IV] shows that the
local marginal polytope of the N(θ) can be parameterized by the set of doubly
stochastic matrices. This is a consequence of the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem.

     As we will see in Section 2.6, the degree-M Bethe permanent permB,M (θ) is
equal to the degree-M Bethe partition function of the S-NFG N(θ), and the proof

configurations in the degree-M covers of the S-NFG N(θ).
     The Sinkhorn free energy function was defined in [Von14]. Here we consider
a variant called the scaled Sinkhorn free energy function that was introduced
in [ACSS21]. These free energy functions are also defined over Γn (θ).

       Note that HscS (γ) can be obtained from HB (γ) by approximating 1−γ(i, j) ·
log(1 − γ(i, j)) by the first-order Taylor series expansion term −γ(i, j) and using
the fact that the sum of all entries of the matrix γ ∈ Γn (θ) equals n.
       In the following, we introduce the degree-M scaled Sinkhorn permanent of θ,
which yields a combinatorial characterization of the scaled Sinkhorn permanent
of θ. (Note that the definition of permscS (θ) in (2.15) is analytical in the sense
that permscS (θ) is given by the solution of an optimization problem.)

Definition 31. Consider the S-NFG N(θ). Let M ∈ Z≥1 . The degree-M scaled
Sinkhorn permanent is defined to be
                                              r                               

where ⊗ denotes the Kronecker product of two matrices and where UM,M is the
matrix of size M × M with all entries equal to 1/M.                                     △

Proposition 32. Consider the S-NFG N(θ). It holds that

                            lim sup permscS,M (θ) = permscS (θ).                    (2.16)
                              M →∞

The limit in (2.16) is visualized in Fig. 1.2 (right).

Proof. See Appendix B.1. (Note that this proof needs some results from Lemma 2
and Proposition 5, which are proven only in the upcoming sections. Of course,
Lemma 2 and Proposition 5 are proven independently of the statement in Propo-
sition 32.)                                                                              

       Except for small values of n, computing permB,M (θ) and permscS,M (θ) appears
to be intractable in general for finite M.8 However, as M goes to infinity, the limit
superior of permB,M (θ) and permscS,M (θ) are equal to permB (θ) and permscS (θ),
respectively, and they can be computed efficiently [Von13b; LSW00].
   8
       More formally, we leave it as an open problem to show that computing permB,M (θ) and
permscS,M (θ) is in the complexity class #P for finite M ∈ Z≥1 .

                                                    x̃2 x̃3            x̃4


                                                       f3    x̃5    f4

                                          Figure 2.4: DE-NFG in Example 33.



     Note that the modified Gibbs entropy function HG′ (γ), the Bethe entropy
function HB (γ), and the scaled Sinkhorn entropy function HscS (γ), as defined in
Definitions 26, 27, and 30, respectively, are well defined for γ ∈ Γn , not just for
γ ∈ Γn (θ). This observation allows us to consider γ ∈ ΓM,n in Proposition 5,
i.e., γ is an element of a set that is independent of θ.


2.5       Double-Edge Normal Factor Graphs (DE-NFGs)

     Now we move to the quantum case. Instead of using the bra-ket notation
of quantum mechanics, we will use standard linear algebra notation similar as
in [LV17; LV20].
     In this section, we review DE-NFGs, which are based on the works in [LV17;
LV20; CV17]. In general, a DE-NFG can contain four types of edges: full double
edges, full single edges, half double edges, and half single edges. For the purposes
of this thesis, it is sufficient to consider only DE-NFGs where all the edges are
full double edges. The reasoning is as follows: similar to Section 2.2, half (dou-
ble/single) edges can be turned into full (double/single) edges by attaching a
dummy 1-valued function node to every half (double/single) edge without chang-
ing any marginals or the partition function. Moreover, DE-NFGs with full single
edges can be turned into DE-NFGs with only full double edges by changing full
single edges into full double edges and suitably reformulating function nodes.
     We start by presenting an example DE-NFG.

Example 33. The DE-NFG in Fig. 2.4 depicts the following factorization

g(x1 , . . . , x5 , x′1 , . . . ,x′5 ) , f1 (x1 , x2 , x3 , x′1 , x′2 , x′3 )
                                · f2 (x1 , x4 , x′1 , x′4 ) · f3 (x2 , x5 , x′2 , x′5 ) · f4 (x3 , x4 , x5 , x′3 , x′4 , x′5 ).

For each double edge e ∈ [5], we associate it with the variable x̃e = (xe , x′e ), where
xe and x′e take values in the same alphabet Xe . Moreover, if e is incident on a
2.5. DOUBLE-EDGE NORMAL FACTOR GRAPHS (DE-NFGS)                                     41


function node f , then x̃e is an argument of the local function f , i.e., both xe and
x′e are arguments of f .
                                                                                     

Definition 34. A DE-NFG N(F , E, X̃ ) consists of the following objects:

  1. A graph (F , E) with vertex set F and edge set E. The set F represents the
      set of function nodes, while E represents the set of full double edges. (As
      mentioned above, every edge e ∈ E will be assumed to be a full double edge
      connecting two function nodes.)
                            Q
  2. An alphabet X̃ ,             X̃e , where X̃e , Xe × Xe is the alphabet of variable
                            e∈E
      x̃e , (xe , x′e ) associated with edge e ∈ E. Without loss of generality, for
      each e ∈ E, we suppose that (0, 0) ∈ X˜e .

For a given DE-NFG N(F , E, X̃ ), we fix the order of the function nodes by F =
{f1 , . . . , f|F | }. Then we make the following definitions.

  4. For every function node f ∈ F , the set ∂f is defined to be the set of edges
      incident on f .

  5. For every edge e ∈ E, the set ∂e represents the set of function nodes that
      are connected to e.

  6. Consider I ⊆ E. The alphabets X̃I and XI are defined to be
                        Y        Y                       Y
                  X̃I ,    X̃e =    (Xe × Xe ),   XI ,      Xe .
                             e∈I         e∈I                       e∈I

      For any mapping h : X̃I → C, we define the following complex-valued square
      matrix:
                                         
                        Ch , h(xI , x′I ) x̃ =(x ,x′ )∈X̃ ∈ C|XI |×|XI |         (2.17)
                                               I    I   I   I


      with row indices xI ∈ XI and column indices x′I ∈ XI .

  7. Consider I ⊆ E. The sets HermXI and PSDXI are defined to be the set of
      Hermitian matrices of size |XI | × |XI | and the set of Hermitian, positive
      semi-definite (PSD) matrices of size |XI | × |XI |, respectively.

  8. An assignment x̃ , (x̃e )e∈E ∈ X̃ is called a configuration of the DE-NFG.
      For each f ∈ F , a configuration x̃ ∈ X̃ induces the vector

                                      x̃∂f = (x̃e )e∈∂f ∈ X̃∂f .
42                                                                 CHAPTER 2. PRELIMINARIES


     9. For each function node f ∈ F , there is a local function associated with
       it, with some slight abuse of notation, also called f . Depending on the
       conditions imposed on f , we distinguish between a strict-sense and a weak-
       sense DE-NFG.

        (a) In the case of a strict-sense DE-NFG, the local function f can be
            an arbitrary mapping from X̃∂f to C satisfying the following property:
            the square matrix

                                                      Cf ∈ PSDX∂f                                  (2.18)

            with row indices x∂f and column indices x′∂f is a complex-valued, Her-
            mitian, positive semi-definite (PSD) matrix. Motivated by the use of
            DE-NFG in quantum information processing (see, e.g., [WBC15]), the
            matrix Cf will be called the Choi-matrix representation of f or the Choi
            matrix associated with f .

        (b) In the case of a weak-sense DE-NFG, we require that Cf ∈ HermX∂f ,
            i.e., the matrix Cf is a complex-valued, Hermitian matrix. However,
            we do not require Cf to be a PSD matrix.

        (c) For each f ∈ F in a strict-sense or weak-sense DE-NFG, the mappings

                                                    uf,ℓf : X̃∂f → C,

     One of the motivations for considering DE-NFGs is that many NFGs with
complex-valued local functions in quantum information processing can be trans-
formed into DE-NFGs. (See [CV17] for further examples and details on this
transformation.)

Example 36 ([LV12; LV17]). Let us consider an NFG in Fig. 2.5(left). Note
that at the end of each edge there is a dot used for denoting the row index of the
matrix w.r.t. the local function. In particular, it describes a quantum system with
two consecutive unitary evolutions:

     • At the first stage, a quantum mechanical system is prepared in a mixed state
         represented by a complex-valued PSD density matrix:
                                                    
                                    ρ , ρ(x1 , x′1 ) x1 ,x′ ∈X1 ∈ PSDX1
                                                           1



         with trace one, where the row is indexed by x1 and the column indexed by
         x′1 .

     • Then the system experiences two consecutive unitary evolutions, which are
         represented by the pair of function nodes U and B, respectively. The associ-
                                     
         ated matrices U(x2 , x1 ) x2 ∈X2 ,x1 ∈X1 with row indices x2 and column indices
                           
         x1 and B(x3 , x2 ) x3 ∈X3 ,x2 ∈X2 with row indices x3 and column indices x2 are
         unitary.

In order to transform this NFG into a DE-NFG, certain modifications are made:

     • The variables xe ∈ Xe and x′e ∈ Xe are merged into the variable

                                    x̃e = (xe , x′e ) ∈ X̃e ,         e ∈ [3].


     • New local functions are defined based on the rearranged variables.
2.5. DOUBLE-EDGE NORMAL FACTOR GRAPHS (DE-NFGS)                                                                    45


The resulting DE-NFG is shown in Fig. 2.5(right). As an example, local function
Ũ is defined to be

                  Ũ(x̃2 , x̃1 ) , U(x2 , x1 ) · U(x′2 , x′1 ),                       x̃2 ∈ X̃2 , x̃1 ∈ X̃1 .

One can verify that the associated Choi-matrix representation CŨ is a PSD matrix
with row indices (x2 , x1 ) and column indices (x′2 , x′1 ).                                                       

Proof. The proof for each statement in the lemma is listed as follows.

      1. The statement in Item 1 is a consequence of Assumption 38 and a variant
            of Schur’s product theorem, which states that the entry-wise product of two
            PSD matrices is again a PSD matrix.11

      2. The statement in Item 2 is a straightforward result of Item 1.

      3. The statement in Item 3 is a consequence of the SPA message update rules.

                               Zf (µ) ∈ R≥0 ,             f ∈ F,

Proposition 43. A sufficient condition for ZB,SPA (N, µ) ∈ R>0 for all SPA fixed-
point message vector µ is that for each f ∈ F in N, the associated Choi-matrix
representation Cf is a positive definite matrix.

Proof. See Appendix A.2.                                                           

Proposition 44. For any cycle-free DE-NFG N, the SPA has a unique fixed-
point message vector µ, and the SPA-based Bethe partition function gives the
exact value of the partition function, i.e.,

                          ∗
                         ZB,SPA (N) = ZB,SPA (N, µ) = Z(N).

Proof. The proof is straightforward and thus is omitted here.                      

     Although formal Definitions 15 and 16 could be potentially extended from S-
NFGs to DE-NFGs, there is a challenge in dealing with the multi-valued nature
of the complex logarithm function. We leave this issue for future research.
     In conclusion, this section provides interesting insights and remarks regarding
the DE-NFGs and their associated SPA. In the following, we summarize these
points:

     1. For each DE-NFG, the SPA fixed point messages are utilized to define the
       Bethe partition function, which serves as an approximation of the partition
       function.

     2. For any strict-sense DE-NFG N, both the partition function Z(N) and the
                                 ∗
       Bethe partition function ZB,SPA (N) are non-negative real-valued functions.

     3. Each S-NFG can be viewed as a special case of a DE-NFG. This observa-
       tion indicates that the concepts, techniques, and theoretical results that are
       developed for DE-NFGs, can be applied to S-NFGs as well.


2.6       Finite Graph Covers

     This section reviews the concept of finite graph covers of a factor graph in
general [HA96; KLVW07; Von13a].

Definition 45. A graph Ĝ = (V̂, Ê) with vertex set V̂ and edge set Ê is the cover
of a graph G = (V, E) if there exists a graph homomorphism π : V̂ → V such
2.6. FINITE GRAPH COVERS                                                                            51


that for each v ∈ V and v̂ ∈ π −1 (v), where π −1 is the pre-image of v under the
mapping π, the neighborhood of v̂, i.e., the set of all vertices in V̂ adjacent to v̂,
is mapped bijectively onto the neighborhood of v, i.e., the set of all vertices in V
adjacent to v. Given a cover Ĝ = (V̂, Ê), if there is a positive integer M such
that |π −1 (v)| = M for all v ∈ V, then Ĝ is called an M-cover.                                    △

    Without loss of generality, we can denote the vertex set of an M-cover by
                                                                        
V̂ , V × [M] and define the mapping π : V̂ → V to be such that π (v, m) = v
for all (v, m) ∈ V̂. Consider an edge in the original graph connecting nodes v and
v ′ , and the corresponding edge in the M-cover connecting (v, m) and (v ′ , m′ ). It
holds that
                                                        
                                  π (v, m) , π (v ′ , m′ ) = (v, v ′).

For every edge e = (v, v ′) in the original graph, there exists a permutation σe ∈
S[M ] associated with the corresponding edges in the M-cover such that the set of
the corresponding edges can be represented by the following set:
                               n
                                               ′
                                                                   o
                                    (v, m), v , σe (m)        m ∈ [M] .

This observation motivates the following definition.

Definition 46. Given an NFG N = (F , E, X ), we define N̂M to be the set of all
M-covers. Each M-cover N̂ = (F̂ , Ê) consists of the vertex set F̂ , F × [M] and
the edge set Ê, where
                       n                                                         o
                Ê ,        fi , mi , fj , σe (mi )      e = (fi , fj ) ∈ E, mi ∈ [M]

                                                              |E|
for some collection of permutations (σe )e∈E ∈ S[M ] .                                              △

       For a given graph G, we consider all graphs in GM to be distinct graphs, despite
the fact that some graphs in the set GM might be isomorphic to each other. (See
also the comments on labeled graph covers after [Von13a, Definition 19].)
       Let N be some S-NFG or DE-NFG. It is straightforward to define the set N̂M of
M-covers N̂ of N. (For a technical description, see [Von13a].) More importantly,
for finite graph covers of DE-NFGs, the double edges are permuted together13
  13
       A graph-cover definition based on splitting the two edges comprising a double edge could be con-
sidered. However, the resulting graph covers would not be immediately relevant for analyzing the SPA
in the way that it is formulated. We leave it for future research.

double edges.                                                                      

represents the average (more precisely, the arithmetic mean) of Z N̂ over all
N̂ ∈ N̂M , where N̂M is the set of all M-covers of N (see Definition 46).          △

Theorem 49 [(Von13a]). Consider some S-NFG N. It holds that

                              lim sup ZB,M (N) = ZB (N).
                               M →∞

Remark 50. Consider the S-NFG N(θ) defined in Definition 22. Vontobel in [Von13b,
Definition 38] showed that
                                                 
                             ZB,M N(θ) = permB,M θ ,

where the degree-M Bethe permanent is defined in Definition 28. Also the limit (2.14)
in Theorem 49 is equivalent to the limit proven in Proposition 29 in this case.

   Given the fact that DE-NFGs are in many ways a natural generalization of
S-NFGs, one wonders if there is a characterization of the Bethe partition function
of a DE-NFG in terms of the partition functions of its graph covers analogous to
Theorem 49, which motivates the following conjecture. Note that the right-hand
                                                             ∗
side of the expression in the following conjecture features ZB,SPA (N), not ZB (N),
because only the former is defined for DE-NFGs.

Conjecture 51. Consider some DE-NFG N. It holds that
                                              ∗
                          lim sup ZB,M (N) = ZB,SPA (N).                    (2.29)
                             M →∞

                                                                                  

   As already mentioned in the introduction, the proof of Theorem 49 heavily
relies on the method of types and the primal formulation of the Bethe partition
function. However, the method of types does not seem to be useful for proving
Conjecture 51, and it is unclear how to generalize the primal formulation of
the Bethe partition function, i.e., Definition 16, to the case of DE-NFGs, so
novel techniques have to be developed. In Theorem 91 in Section 6, we prove
Conjecture 51 for a class of DE-NFGs satisfying an easily checkable condition.
We conjecture that it holds for more general DE-NFGs.
   In the following, we provide promising numerical results supporting Conjec-
ture 51.

Chapter 3


Finite-Graph-Covers-based
Bound for the Permanent of a
Non-negative Square Matrix

   In this chapter, we study the degree-M Bethe permanent and degree-M
Sinkhorn permanent, whose definitions are motivated by the degree-M finite
graph covers of the associated S-NFG, as defined in Section 2.3. In particu-
lar, in Section 3.1, we present the recursions of the coefficients in expressions of
the degree-M Bethe permanent and degree-M Sinkhorn permanent. Based on
these recursions, in Section 3.2, we prove the main results in Section 1.1.4, i.e., we
bound the permanent via its degree-M Bethe permanent and degree-M Sinkhorn
permanent. Then in Section 3.3, we gives asymptotic expressions for the coef-
ficients in the degree-M Bethe permanent and degree-M Sinkhorn permanent.
Finally, in Section 3.4, we focus on the case where M = 2 and connects some of
the results in this chapter with results in other papers.



3.1    Recursions for the
       Coefficients CM,n (γ), CB,M,n (γ), CscS,M,n (γ)

lemmas that will help us in the proofs of Theorems 3 and 4 in the next section.
These latter lemmas are recursions (in M) on the coefficients CM,n (γ), CB,M,n (γ),
and CscS,M,n (γ). Note that these recursions on the coefficients CM,n (γ), CB,M,n (γ),

(See also the discussion in Example 62).

Proof of Lemma 2.

  1. The expression in (1.5) follows from
                                                                             !M
                                   M           X Y                         
                         perm(θ)        =                       θ i, σ(i)

58                        CHAPTER 3. BOUND FOR THE MATRIX PERMANENT


       where step (a) follows from expanding the right-hand side of the first line,
       along with using the Birkhoff–von Neumann theorem and Eq. (3.1).

       While it is clear that CM,n (γ) ≥ 0 for all γ ∈ ΓM,n (θ), note that the first
       inequality in (1.8) in Theorem 3 (proven later in this thesis) can be used to
       show that CM,n (γ) > 0 for all γ ∈ ΓM,n (θ).

                                                                              ′
     2. Eq. (1.6) follows from [Von13a, Lemma 29], where the role of the set BM
       in [Von13a, Lemma 29] is played by the set Γ′M,n (θ) in this thesis.

       The set Γ′M,n (θ) is defined as follows. Namely, as mentioned in Section 2.4,
       the paper [Von13b, Section IV] shows that the local marginal polytope of
       N(θ) can be parameterized by the set of doubly stochastic matrices, more
       precisely it can be parameterized by Γn (θ). For arbitrary M ∈ Z≥1 , the
       set Γ′M,n (θ) ⊆ Γn (θ) is then defined to be the parameterization of the set
       of pseudo-marginal vectors in the local marginal polytope that are M-cover
       lift-realizable pseudo-marginal vectors. (For the definition of M-cover lift-
       realizable pseudo-marginal vectors, see [Von13a, Section VI].)

       We claim Γ′M,n (θ) = ΓM,n (θ). Indeed, it follows from the construction of
       pseudo-marginal vectors based on M-covers that Γ′M,n (θ) ⊆ ΓM,n (θ), and
       it follows from the construction in the proof of [Von13a, Lemma 29] that
       for every element of ΓM,n (θ) the corresponding pseudo-marginal vector is
       M-cover lift-realizable, i.e., Γ′M,n (θ) ⊇ ΓM,n (θ).

     3. Eq. (1.7) follows from [Fri79, Theorem 2.1].

be useful to formulate the upcoming results.

    of the column indices of columns where γ contains exactly one 1 and 0s
    otherwise.

 2. We define r(γ) , |R|. One can verify the following statements:

      • |R| = |C|.

        tic matrix of size r(γ) × r(γ).

where the approximation is up to o(k1 + k2 ) in the exponent.
                                                                                            
   Similar statements can be made for CB,k1 +k2 ,n γ (k1 ,k2) and CscS,k1 +k2 ,n γ (k1 ,k2 )
in Fig. 3.2 and Fig. 3.3, respectively. Namely, for k1 , k2 ∈ Z≥0 , the value of
                        
CB,k1 +k2 ,n γ (k1 ,k2 ) equals the sum of weighted directed paths from the vertex la-
                                                                     
beled “CB,0,n γ (0,0) ” to the vertex labeled “CB,k1+k2 ,n γ (k1 ,k2 ) ”, and
                          
CscS,k1 +k2 ,n γ (k1 ,k2 ) equals the sum of weighted directed paths from the vertex
                                                                           
labeled “CscS,0,n γ (0,0) ” to the vertex labeled “CscS,k1 +k2 ,n γ (k1 ,k2) ”. (Here, the
weight of a directed path is given by the product of the weights of the directed
edges in the path.) In this context, recall from Proposition 5 that
                                                                           
                               (k1 ,k2 )                           (k1 ,k2 )
               CB,k1 +k2 ,n γ              ≈ exp (k1 + k2 ) · HB γ              ,
                                                                            
              CscS,k1 +k2 ,n γ (k1 ,k2 ) ≈ exp (k1 + k2 ) · HscS γ (k1 ,k2 ) ,

where the approximation is up to o(k1 + k2 ) in the exponent.                              

   In the case of the scaled Sinkhorn permanent, the “worst-case matrix” is
given by the all-one matrix not just for n = 2, but for all n ∈ Z≥2 , i.e., the ratio
 perm(θ)
permscS (θ)
            = en · nn!n for θ = 1n×n is the furthest away from the value of the ratio
 perm(θ)
permscS (θ)
            = en for diagonal matrices θ for all n ∈ Z≥2 .

3.2      Bounding the Permanent of θ

     In this section, we first prove Theorem 3 with the help of Lemmas 60 and 61,
where, in order for Lemmas 60 and 61 to be useful, we need to find lower and
upper bounds on perm(γ̂R,C ) and on perm(γ), respectively. Afterwards, we use
Theorem 3 to prove Theorem 4.


3.2.1    Bounding the Ratio perm(θ)/permB,M (θ)

     In this subsection, we first prove the inequalities in (1.8) in Theorem 3 that
give bounds on the ratio CM,n (γ)/CB,M,n (γ). Then, based on these bounds,
we prove the inequalities in (1.10) in Theorem 4 that give bounds on the ratio
perm(θ)/permB,M (θ).
