3.3    Asymptotic Expressions for the Coefficients CM,n(γ),
       CB,M,n (γ), CscS,M,n (γ)

   In this section, we prove Proposition 5. Whereas Definitions 26, 27, and 30
give an analytical definition of the entropy functions HG′ , HB , and HscS , respec-
tively, the expressions in Eqs. (1.12)–(1.14) in Proposition 5 give a combinatorial
characterization of these entropy functions. The proof of Proposition 5 is based
on standard results of the method of types and considerations that are similar to
the considerations in [Von13a, Sections IV and VII].

Chapter 4


Loop-calculus Transform (LCT)

   Loop calculus, as defined by Chertkov and Chernyak [CC06; CC07] and further
developed by Mori [Mor15b], can be formulated as a certain holographic trans-
form [AM11] applied to an S-NFG, and will henceforth be called the loop-calculus
transform (LCT). After applying the LCT to an S-NFG, the partition function
equals the sum of the Bethe partition function and some correction terms, where
the sum of the correction terms are related to a weighted sum over all generalized
loops in the S-NFG.
   In this chapter, we introduce the LCT for both S-NFGs and DE-NFGs. The
LCT for DE-NFGs has been discussed by Cao in [Cao21, Theorem 2.21]. Let
us make a comparison between the LCT proposed in [Mor15b; Cao21] and our
proposed LCT.

   • Mori in [Mor15b] considered S-NFGs and defined the LCT based on the
     SPA fixed-point message vector that consists of positive-valued entries only.
     Note that he did not define the LCT for the SPA fixed-point message vector
     that consists of at least one zero-valued entry.

   • Cao in [Cao21] considered DE-NFGs and defined the LCT for DE-NFGs
     based on the SPA fixed-point message vector where the entries are allowed
     to take values in the field of complex numbers. However, he only stated
     the constraints that the LCT has to satisfy, and he did not provide explicit
     expressions that enable us to applying the LCT for DE-NFGs.

   • In this section, we explicitly define the LCT for both S-NFGs and DE-
     NFGs. Our proposed LCT is defined based on the SPA fixed-point message

                                        77
78                     CHAPTER 4. LOOP-CALCULUS TRANSFORM (LCT)

4.1. LCT FOR S-NFGS                                                                                     79


      vector whose entries can take values in the field of complex numbers, i.e.,
      the SPA fixed-point message vector is allowed to have zero-valued entries.
      Our proposed LCT generalizes the results in [Mor15b; Cao21].

   • In Chapter 6, we use the LCT for DE-NFGs to prove the graph-cover the-
      orem, i.e., Conjecture 51 for a class of DE-NFGs that satisfy an easily
      checkable condition.


4.1    LCT for S-NFGs

   Before giving the general definition of the LCT for S-NFGs, we consider a
specific S-NFG N and assume that a part of it looks as shown in Fig. 4.1a. Let
µ be some SPA fixed-point message vector for N. The LCT is a sequence of
modifications to the original S-NFG that leave the partition function unchanged.
This is done by applying opening-the-box and closing-the box operations [Loe04]
as follows.

  1. The S-NFG in Fig. 4.1b is obtained from the S-NFG in Fig. 4.1a by applying
      suitable opening-the-box operations [Loe04]. Importantly, the new function
      nodes hi are based on µ and are defined in such a way that the partition
      function remains unchanged.

      For example, for the edge 1 that connects the function nodes fi and fj , as
      shown in Fig. 4.1a, we introduce the function nodes h3 and h8 with the local
      functions h3 : X1 × X̊1 → C and h8 : X1 × X̊1 → C, where X̊1 , X1 . The
      partition function of the S-NFG remains unchanged because h3 and h8 are
      defined such that the following equalities hold
              X
                     h3 (x1,fi , x̊1 ) · h8 (x1,fj , x̊1 ) = [x1,fi = x1,fj ],   x1,fi , x1,fj ∈ X1 .
          x̊1 ∈X̊1


  2. The S-NFG in Fig. 4.1c is essentially the same as the S-NFG in Fig. 4.1b.

  3. The S-NFG in Fig. 4.1d is obtained from the S-NFG in Fig. 4.1c by apply-
      ing suitable closing-the-box operations [Loe04] to the S-NFG in Fig. 4.1c.
      Consider the dashed boxes on the left-hand side in Fig. 4.1c. Its exterior
      function is defined to be the sum, over the internal variables, of the prod-
      uct of the internal local functions. Replacing this dashed box by a single
80                                       CHAPTER 4. LOOP-CALCULUS TRANSFORM (LCT)


           function node that represents this exterior function is known as closing-the-
           box operation [Loe04]. For example, the local function f˚i with the set of
           adjacent edges ∂fi = {1, 2, 3} is defined to be
                                                      X
                   f˚i (x̊1 , x̊2 , x̊3 ) ,                         fi (x1,fi , x2,fi , x3,fi ) · h3 (x1,fi , x̊1 )
                                        x1,fi ∈X1 ,x2,fi ∈X2 ,x3,fi ∈X2

                                                                     · h2 (x2,fi , x̊2 ) · h4 (x3,fi , x̊3 ).

           The partition function of the S-NFG remains unchanged due to the way
           that f˚ is defined.

         Let us now give the general definition of the LCT for an S-NFG N that we
use in this thesis, along with stating its main properties.

Definition 72. Consider some S-NFG N and let µ be some SPA fixed-point
message vector. In the following, for each e = (fi , fj ) ∈ E, we use the short-hand

     1. (generalization of the first step above) For every edge e ∈ E, we do the
           following.

                 simplify the notation, and without loss of generality, this special element
                 will be called 0 for all edges, i.e., we suppose that 0 ∈ Xe .

               • We define the alphabet X̊e , Xe .1

                 by the following alternating sequence of edges and function nodes: an

                 with associated variable x̊e ∈ X̊e , a function node Me,fj , and an edge
                 with associated variable xe,fj ∈ Xe . Here:

Remark 73. The following remarks provide insights into the limitations and
characteristics of the LCT in different scenarios.
84                          CHAPTER 4. LOOP-CALCULUS TRANSFORM (LCT)


     1. There are S-NFGs where it is not possible to obtain Me,fi and Me,fj for
       e = (fi , fj ) ∈ E based on the SPA fixed-point messages. Consider a single-
       cycle S-NFG in Item 3 in Remark 21. The SPA fixed-point message vector
       satisfies Ze = 0 for all e ∈ E and thus the LCT does not work due to the
       equations (4.3)–(4.5). The existence of the LCT for this single-cycle S-NFG
       would contradict the non-diagonalizability of the matrix
                                             
                                         1 1
                                             .
                                         0 1

     2. The LCT N̊ of N is in general not an S-NFG. This is because some of the
       local functions of N̊ might take on negative real values. With that, also the
       global function of N̊ might take on negative real values.

     3. In the case of a binary alphabet Xe , the LCT we obtain is the same as the
       one presented in [CC06]. However, for non-binary alphabets Xe , our LCT,
       although inspired by previous works such as [CC06; CC07; Mor15b], is in
       general different. Notably, our approach does not require any assumption of
       non-zeroness on the SPA fixed-point messages.

                                                                                  △

Proposition 74. Consider an S-NFG N and let µ be an SPA fixed-point message
vector for N. The LCT of N, as specified in Definition 72, is denoted by N̊. (Recall
that in Assumption 13, we assume Ze (µ) > 0 for all e ∈ E, which ensures that
the LCT of N is well-defined.) The main properties of N̊ are listed as follows.

     1. The partition function remains the same:
                                                  
                                       Z(N) = Z N̊ .

     2. The SPA-based Bethe partition function of the original S-NFG is equal to
       the global function value of the all-zero configuration of N̊:

                                    ZB,SPA (N, µ) = g̊(0).

     3. For every f ∈ F and every x̊∂f ∈ X̊∂f with exactly one non-zero component,
       i.e.,

                                       wH (x̊∂f ) = 1,

                                       f˚(x̊∂f ) = 0,

    contain any leaves (vertices of degree one). (Note that for the all-zero con-
    figuration x̊ = 0, the subgraph (F , E ′(x̊)) is also considered as a generalized

    for the configuration x̊, then g̊(x̊) = 0. If N is cycle-free and with that also

    configuration and corresponds to a generalized loop.

     We conclude this section with some remarks.

     • Property 2 in Proposition 74 states that the LCT is a reparamerization of
       the global function based on the SPA fixed point message vector µ such that
       the global function evaluated at x̊ = 0 equals the µ-based Bethe partition
       function. The LCT can be viewed as shifting the bulk of the sum over
       the variables of the global function to some known configuration as well as
       keeping the partition function unchanged.

     • Property 6 in Proposition 74 highlights that after applying the LCT on
       N based on the SPA fixed-point message vector µ, one of the SPA fixed-
       point message vectors for the LCT-transformed factor graph N̊ has a simple
       structure. Specifically, this simple SPA fixed-point message vector, denoted
       by µ̊, satisfies

             ZB,SPA (N, µ) = ZB,SPA (N̊, µ̊),
                  µ̊e,fi (x̊e ) = µ̊e,fj (x̊e ) = [x̊e = 0],   x̊e ∈ X̊e , e = (fi , fj ) ∈ E.

       This simple structure partially motivates the proof of the graph-cover the-
       orem in Section 6.


4.2      LCT for DE-NFGs

     The LCT for DE-NFGs has many similarities to the LCT for S-NFGs. There-
fore, we will mostly highlight the differences in this section.

Definition 75. The LCT for DE-NFGs is a natural extension of the LCT for
S-NFG in Definition 72. Further definitions are made for DE-NFGs. For each
edge e ∈ E, we apply the following changes.
4.2. LCT FOR DE-NFGS                                                                                       87


   • The alphabet Xe is replaced by the alphabet X̃e = Xe × Xe .

   • The special element 0 ∈ Xe is replaced by the special element 0̃ , (0, 0) ∈ X˜e .

   • The variable xe ∈ Xe is replaced by the variable x̃e = (xe , x′e ) ∈ X̃e .

4.2. LCT FOR DE-NFGS                                                        89


   Most of the LCT properties for S-NFG can be straightforwardly extended to
the case of DE-NFG. We note that applying the LCT to a strict-sense DE-NFG
or a weak-sense DE-NFG yields in another weak-sense DE-NFG, which is similar
to the property of the LCT for S-NFG as discussed in Item 2 in Remark 73.
Chapter 5


Symmetric Subspace Transform
(SST)

   In this chapter, we introduce the symmetric-subspace transform (SST) for
both S-NFG and DE-NFG. The SST provides a different perspective to under-
stand the M-covers of S-NFGs or DE-NFGs. While this transformation has been
applied in quantum physics [WBC15; Har13], to the best of our knowledge, it is
the first time to introduce the SST in the factor-graph literature.
   The developments in this chapter were motivated by the work of Wood et
al. [WBC15], where, in terms of the language of the present paper, they trans-
formed a certain integral into the average partition function of some NFGs, with
the average taken over double covers. (Although [WBC15] focus on double cov-
ers, it is clear that their results can be extended to general M-covers.) However,
in contrast to [WBC15], our approach takes the opposite direction. We express
the average partition function of a given NFG in terms of some integral, where
the average is over all M-covers of the considered NFG.
   The developments in this chapter were partially also motivated by the results
presented in [Von16].
   Let N be some S-NFG or DE-NFG. In Definition 48, the degree-M Bethe
partition function of N is
                                          rD
                                                      E
                             ZB,M (N) =   M
                                               Z N̂                 .
                                                           N̂∈N̂M

(See also Definition 46.) Our particular interest lies in the limit superior of this
quantity as M → ∞, as stated in Conjecture 51. Toward proving Conjecture 51,
                               M                      
we aim to reformulate ZB,M (N)    (or equivalently Z N̂ N̂∈N̂ ) as an integral
                                                                        M



                                          90
5.1. SPECIFYING AN M -COVER OF AN S-NFG                                                   91


that can be analyzed in the limit M → ∞. This approach allows us to study the
behavior of the Bethe partition function for large values of M.
     In order to introduce the approach, we consider a specific example of an S-
NFG. In this example, the mathematical formalism that we will present is general,
but we will illustrate it under a particularly simple setup. Specifically, we will
focus a part of the S-NFG looks like Fig. 5.1a and set Xe = {0, 1} and M = 2.



5.1       Specifying an M-Cover of an S-NFG

     The following construction process defines an M-cover of the original S-NFG
N.

Definition 77. The construction process for an M-cover of N is outlined as
follows (see also Definition 46):

     1. For each edge e ∈ E, we specify a permutation σe ∈ S[M ] . We collect all
                                                               |E|
        these permutations into a vector σ , (σe )e∈E ∈ S[M ] .

     2. For each function node f ∈ F , we draw M copies of f . Each copy of function
        node f is associated with a collection of sockets and variables. Specifically,
        if e ∈ ∂f , then the m-th copy of f has a socket with associated variable
        denoted by xe,f,m .

     3. For every edge e = (fi , fj ) ∈ E, we specify how the sockets corresponding to
        (xe,fi ,m )m∈[M ] , are connected to the sockets corresponding to (xe,fj ,m )m∈[M ] .
        In this thesis, the connection is determined based on the chosen permutation
        σe ∈ S[M ] . Specifically, the socket corresponding to xe,fi ,m is connected to the
        socket corresponding to xe,fj ,σe (m) for m ∈ [M]. The possible cases for this
        connection are illustrated in Figs. 5.1b and 5.1c, considering that |S[M ] | =
        2! = 2 for M = 2.

     4. The resulting M-cover is denoted by N̂M,σ .

                                                                                          △
                                                         
     In order to simplify the notation, let ΣM , Σ , Σ(e) e be the random vector
consisting of independent and uniformly distributed (i.u.d.) random permuta-

5.2          Replacement of M-Cover S-NFG by another S-NFG

         In order to proceed, it is convenient to replace each M-cover N̂M,σ with an
S-NFG denoted by N̂M,Pσ , which satisfies the property
                                                           

Before providing the general definition of N̂M,Pσ , we briefly discuss this replace-
ment in terms of the illustrated example. Instead of working with the S-NFG in
Fig. 5.1b, we will work with the S-NFG in Fig. 5.1d. Similarly, instead of working
with the S-NFG in Fig. 5.1c, we will work with the S-NFG in Fig. 5.1e.
                                E
Definition 78. For a given σ ∈ S[M ] , the S-NFG N̂M,Pσ is defined as follows:

     1. For every f ∈ F , we draw M copies of f . Each copy of f is associated with
           a collection of sockets and variables. Specifically, if e ∈ ∂f , the m-th copy
           of f has a socket with an associated variable xe,f,m . (This step is the same
           as Step 2 in Definition 77.)

     2. For every edge e = (fi , fj ) ∈ E, we draw a function node Pe,σe . This
           node is connected to the sockets associated with the collection of variables
           (xe,fi ,m )m∈[M ] and to the sockets associated with the collection of variables
           (xe,fj ,m )m∈[M ] . The local function Pe,σe : XeM × XeM → {0, 1} is defined to be

Definition 79. The construction of N̂M,avg is essentially the same as the con-
struction of N̂M,Pσ for any σ in Definition 79, but with the following modifications
and additions. Namely, for every e ∈ E, the permutation σe is not fixed, but a
                                                                
variable. With this, the function Pe,σe xe,fi,[M ] , xe,fj ,[M ] is now considered to be
a function not only of xe,fi,[M ] and xe,fj ,[M ], but also of σe . Graphically, this is
done as follows:

     • For every e ∈ E, we draw a function node pΣ(e) .

     • For every e ∈ E, we draw an edge that connects Pe,σe and pΣ(e) and that
       represents the variable σe . Recall pΣ(e) (σe ) = (M!)−1 for all e ∈ E and
       σe ∈ S[M ] , as defined in (5.1).

The Graph-Cover Theorem for
Special DE-NFG

   In this section, we present a proof for Conjecture 51, i.e., the graph-cover
theorem, for a class of DE-NFGs satisfying an easily checkable condition. In
Conjecture 51, we conjecture that for some DE-NFG N, the limit in (2.29) holds
   This section is organized as follows.

  1. Application of the LCT: We begin by applying the LCT to a DE-NFG
                                                   ∗
     N, based on an SPA fixed point that achieves ZB,SPA (N). The resulting
     factor graph is denoted by N̊. Recall in Proposition 76, we establish that
                        
     ZB,M (N) = ZB,M N̊ for any M ∈ Z≥1 .

  2. Limit Evaluation: In order to establish the limit stated in (2.29), we
     need to evaluate ZB,M (N) as M → ∞. We propose a checkable sufficient
     condition based on the local functions in the LCT-transformed DE-NFG N̊,
                                        ∗
     which ensures that lim ZB,M N̊) = ZB,SPA (N).
                         M →∞


   The considered setup is given as follows.

   • Consider an arbitrary DE-NFG N.

   • Assume that among all fixed-point SPA message vectors for N, we obtain a
     vector µ that achieves the maximum value of ZB,SPA (N, µ), i.e.,

                              ∗
             ZB,SPA (N, µ) = ZB,SPA (N) ∈ R≥0 ,    Ze (µ) > 0,    e ∈ E.

     (See also Definition 40 and Assumption 42.)

Chapter 7


Summary and Open Problems

   In this chapter, we summarize this thesis and present open problems that are
left for future research.



7.1    Degree-M-Bethe-permanent-based bounds for
       the permanent of a non-negative square matrix

   In Chapter 2, we have introduced a class of S-NFGs such that the partition
function of each S-NFG equals the permanent of a non-negative square matrix.
We have also introduced the degree-M Bethe and scaled Sinkhorn permanents,
which are based on the degree-M covers of the underlying S-NFG.
   In Chapter 3, we have shown that it is possible to bound the permanent of
a non-negative matrix by its degree-M Bethe and scaled Sinkhorn permanents,
thereby, among other statements, proving a conjecture in [Von13b]. A key result
are the recursive expressions for the coefficients appearing in the expressions for
the M-th power of the degree-M Bethe permanent and the M-th power of the
degree-M scaled Sinkhorn permanent.
   While the results in Chapter 3 do not yield any new numerical techniques for
bounding the permanent of a non-negative square matrix, they can, besides being
of inherent combinatorial interest, potentially be used to obtain new analytical
results about the Bethe and the Sinkhorn approximation of the permanent, sim-
ilar to the results in the paper [NV22]. (See also the discussion at the end of
Section 1.1.4.)
   Some open problems about the topic studied in Chapter 3 include the follow-

                                       107
108                             CHAPTER 7. SUMMARY AND OPEN PROBLEMS


ing:

   • Our proofs in Chapter 3 used some rather strong results from [Sch98; Gur11;
       AR19; Ego81; Fal81]. We leave it as an open problem to find “more basic”
       proofs for some of the inequalities that were established in this thesis.

   • One can verify that proving the first inequality in (1.2) for all θ is equiv-
       alent to proving HG′ (γ) ≥ HB (γ) for all γ ∈ Γn . Note that proving the
       first inequality in (1.2) for all θ is also equivalent to proving Schrijver’s in-
       equality [Sch98]. We leave it as an open problem to prove HG′ (γ) ≥ HB (γ)
       for all γ ∈ Γn via an approach different from Schrijver’s inequality and its
       variations.

   • It was remarked that both the second inequality in (1.10) and the first
       inequality in (1.11) are not tight. We leave it as an open problem to find
       tighter bounds, or even tight bounds.

   • Recently, Huang and Vontobel [HV24] extended Vontobel’s results in [Von13b]
       by considering a class of bipartite S-NFGs where each local function is de-
       fined based on a (possibly different) multi-affine homogeneous real stable
       polynomial. The S-NFGs considered in [HV24] can be seen as an extension
       of the class of S-NFGs considered in Chapter 3. Generalizing the results in
       Section 1.1.4 to the S-NFGs discussed in [HV24] remains an open problem
       for future research.


7.2      Characterizing the Bethe Partition Function of
         Double-Edges Factor Graphs via Graph Covers

      In Chapter 2, we have also introduced the general definitions of both S-NFGs
and DE-NFGs. The latter are highly relevant in the field of quantum information
processing. We have defined the SPA and finite graph covers for both S-NFGs
and DE-NFGs. A key difference between S-NFGs and DE-NFGs is that S-NFGs
consist of non-negative local functions only, while DE-NFGs have complex-valued
local functions that satisfy some positive semi-definiteness constraints. We have
considered a special SPA for DE-NFGs, where the messages are initialized to
satisfy certain positive semi-definiteness constraints.
7.2. THE GRAPH-COVER THEOREM FOR SPECIAL DE-FGS                                  109


   In Chapters 4 and 5, we have defined the LCT and the SST for both S-NFGs
and DE-NFGs. Our proposed LCT does not have the limitation on non-zero
components in SPA fixed-point messages, which extends the LCT proposed by
Mori [Mor15b]. The SST is a novel contribution to the graphical models literature
and has broader applications beyond proving the main results of this thesis.
   In Chapter 6, we have proven the graph-cover theorem, i.e., a combinatorial
characterization of the Bethe partition function in terms of finite graph covers, for
a class of DE-NFGs that satisfy an easily checkable condition. The proof is based
on analyzing the finite graphs cover of loop-calculus transformed DE-NFGs.
   The graph-cover theorem for DE-NFGs, beyond those satisfying the easily
checkable condition stated in Chapter 6, remains an open problem. In Chapter 2,
we have supported the graph-cover conjecture for DE-NFGs with numerical re-
sults on small DE-NFGs. We also note that the SST provides a different perspec-
tive to understand finite graph covers, and we hope that the SST can be used for
proving the graph-cover theorem for more general DE-NFGs.

      The proof can be viewed as a generalization of the main idea in the proof [YFW05,

      For each e ∈ ∂f and f ∈ F , the fixed-point message vector µe,f satisfies the

A.2. PROOF OF PROPOSITION 43                                                                                                  113


Without loss of generality, we consider the following setup.

   • The edge 1 connects function nodes f1 and f2 .

   • ∂f1 = {1, 2, . . . , |∂f1 |};

Lemma 93. Let σ1 , σ2 ∈ S[n] be fixed. Define the matrix
                                            1         
                                    γ,        Pσ1 +Pσ2 .
                                            2
Let the graph G(σ1 , σ2 ) be a bipartite graph with two times n vertices: n vertices
on the LHS and n vertices on the RHS, each of them labeled by [n]. Let there be
an edge (i, j) connecting the i-th vertex on the LHS with the j-th vertex on the
                                       
RHS if γ(i, j) = 21 . Let c G(σ1 , σ2 ) be the number of cycles in G(σ1 , σ2 ).
      The matrix γ has the following properties:

  1. γ ∈ Γ2,n .

  2. For every i ∈ [n], the i-th row γ(i, :) of γ has one of the following two
        possible compositions:

          • One entry is equal to 1; n−1 entries are equal to 0.

          • Two entries are equal to 21 ; n−2 entries are equal to 0.

The graph G(σ1 , σ2 ) has the following properties:

  1. The edge set of G(σ1 , σ2 ) forms a collection of disjoint cycles of even length.

  2. Every cycle in G(σ1 , σ2 ) of length 2L corresponds to a cycle of length L in
        the cycle notation of σ1 ◦ σ2−1 .
                  
  3. c G(σ1 , σ2 ) = c(σ1 , σ2 ).

Proof. The derivation of the properties of γ is rather straightforward and is there-
fore omitted. The properities of G(σ1 , σ2 ) follow from the following observations:

   • Because G(σ1 , σ2 ) is a bipartite graph, all cycles must have even length.

   • Let i ∈ [n]. If σ1 (i) = σ2 (i), then the i-th vertex on the LHS has no indicent
        edges.

C.1      Details of the LCT for S-NFGs

   Given |Xe | ∈ Z≥1 , without loss of generality, we suppose that Xe = X̊e =
{0, 1, . . . , |Xe | − 1}. We have to verify that the the functions Me,fi and Me,fj
satisfy (4.1), i.e.,
      X
             Me,fi (xe,fi , x̊e ) · Me,fj (xe,fj , x̊e ) = [xe,fi = xe,fj ],              xe,fi , xe,fj ∈ Xe .
       x̊e

We consider the following four cases:

  1. For xe,fi = 0, xe,fj = 0, we obtain the constraint

      ζe,fi · µe,fi (0) · ζe,fj · µe,fj (0)
                           X                                                                   
                      +            ζe,fi · χe,fi · −µe,fj (x̊e ) · ζe,fj · χe,fj · −µe,fi (x̊e ) = 1.

      Using (4.5) and (4.8), and multiplying both size by Ze , we obtain the equiv-
      alent constraint
                                                      X
                         µe,fi (0) · µe,fj (0) +                   µe,fj (x̊e ) · µe,fi (x̊e ) = Ze .
                                                   x̊e : x̊e 6=0

      Clearly, this constraint is satisfied since the left-hand side is just the defini-
      tion of Ze .

  2. For xe,fi = 0, xe,fj ∈ Xe \ {0}, we obtain the constraint
                                                             X                                            
         ζe,fi · µe,fi (0) · ζe,fj · µe,fj (xe,fj ) +                     ζe,fi · χe,fi · −µe,fj (x̊e )

     The expression in the parentheses equals zero because of (4.11), and so this
     constraint is satisfied.

 3. For xe,fi ∈ Xe \{0}, xe,fj = 0, we obtain similar expressions as for the second

                    · 1 + δe,fi · ǫe,fj + δe,fj · ǫe,fi + ǫe,fi · ǫe,fj · Ze · 1 − βe (0) = 0.

      We claim that this constraint is satisfied. This follows from the above two
      expressions being equal to zero. For the first parenthetical expression, this
      observation follows from (4.9). For the second parenthetical expression, this
      observation follows from the following considerations. If βe (0) = 1, the
      second parenthetical expression equals

                                 1 + δe,fi · ǫe,fj + δe,fj · ǫe,fi = 0,

      where the last equality follows from (4.12). Now we consider βe (0) 6= 1.
      Because the product of the left-hand sides of (4.10) and (4.11) must equal
      the product of the right-hand sides of (4.10) and (4.11), we get
                                                                              
      δe,fi · δe,fj + δe,fi · Ze · 1 − βe (0) · ǫe,fj + δe,fj · Ze · 1 − βe (0) · ǫe,fi

                                   + (Ze )2 · 1 − βe (0) · ǫe,fi · ǫe,fj = µe,fi (0) · µe,fj (0).

      Using (4.9) and µe,fi (0) · µe,fj (0) = Ze · βe (0), we obtain
                                                                   
       Ze + δe,fi · Ze · 1 − βe (0) ·ǫe,fj + δe,fj · Ze · 1 − βe (0) · ǫe,fi
                                                            2
                                      + (Ze )2 · 1 − βe (0) · ǫe,fi · ǫe,fj = Ze · βe (0).
                                                               
      Subtracting Ze · βe (0) and then dividing Ze · 1 − βe (0) from both sides,
      results in
                                                                  
               1 + δe,fi · ǫe,fj + δe,fj · ǫe,fi + Ze · 1 − βe (0) · ǫe,fi · ǫe,fj = 0.

      This is the promised result.

    In this appendix, we will use the Gamma function, i.e., Γ(z) ,                    0

for z ∈ C with Re(z) > 0. Note that Γ(z) = (z − 1)! if z is a positive-valued
integer. However, we will use the Gamma function Γ also with non-integral real
arguments. Moreover, let Sn be the (n−1)-dimensional area of the unit sphere in
Rn . It holds that Sn = 2 · π n/2 /Γ(n/2) (see, e.g., [Fol01]).
    The following lemma contains a useful technical result.

Lemma 103. The following properties for binomial coefficients hold.

  1. Consider the case where
                                                                                     X
                           ki ∈ Z≥1 ,       i ∈ [n],           M ∈ Z≥1 ,                     ki ≤ M.
                                                                                     i∈[n]

      The following inequality holds
                                                               Y M                               
                                                                                         M
                                                                                 ≥       P           ,           (E.5)
                                                                         ki                    ki
                                                               i∈[n]
                                                                                       i∈[n]
                                                                                                   
                                       M!                                                M
                                        !            ≥                                 P           .           (E.6)
                                     P        Q                                                ki
                                  M−   ki ! ·   (ki !)                                 i∈[n]
                                           i∈[n]                 i∈[n]

      The inequalities (E.5) and (E.6) hold with equalities if and only if there
      exists an integer i′ ∈ [n] such that ki = ki′ for i = i′ and ki = 0 for
      i ∈ [n] ∈ \{i′ }.
                                                                                                                                   145


                                                     M
                                                         
    2. The binomial coefficient                      k
                                                              increases w.r.t. k ∈ Z≥0 when k ≤ M/2 and
         decreases w.r.t. k ∈ Z≥0 when k ≥ M/2.

 Proof. The inequality (E.5) follows from a straightforward generalization of the
 following standard combinatorial results:
                          −1
  M        M             M
       ·          ·
  k1       k2          k1 + k2
                                                                                       
      (k1 + k2 )!       M · (M − 1) · · · (M − k1 + 1) · M · (M − 1) · · · (M − k2 + 1)
  =                  ·
     (k1 !) · (k2 !)                  M · (M − 1) · · · (M − k1 − k2 + 1)
     |     {z       } |                                 {z                              }
           ≥1                                                                     ≥1

  ≥ 1,

 where the inequality holds with equality if and only if at least one of the entries
 in (k1 , k2 ) is zero-valued.
                                                                                                                                     
     There is a combinatorial proof for                           M
                                                                  k1
                                                                           · M
                                                                             k2
                                                                                  ≥      M
                                                                                       k1 +k2
                                                                                                      . The number       M
                                                                                                                         k1
                                                                                                                                  · M
                                                                                                                                    k2

 equals the number of ways to finish the following procedure: first, choose k1
 different elements from M different elements; second, put these k1 elements back
 to the M elements; last, choose k2 different elements from these M different
 elements. Therefore, compared with the ways to choose k1 + k2 different elements
 from M different elements, we have more ways to finish the former procedure.
     The proof for the inequality (E.6) and the monotonicity of the binomial coeffi-
 cients as stated in the second property in the lemma statement is straightforward
 and thus it is omitted here.                                                                                                        
